Appendix D
Proofs

This appendix contains the statement and proofs of several propositions used in this paper.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
N
be a positive integer,
\{s_{n}(x)\,|\,n\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\}
be a set of orthonormal, real-valued, differentiable and compactly supported functions that span a subspace of
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathds{R})
, and let
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}
be a matrix whose elements are

\Gamma_{nm}:=\int\differential{x}\derivative{x}s_{n}(x)\derivative{x}s_{m}(x)\quad\forall\,n,m\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}.

(154)

Then
\bm{\Gamma}
is a symmetric and positive-semidefinite matrix.

Proof.

The symmetry of
\bm{\Gamma}
is immediate from the definition of its elements.
By definition, an
N
-by-
N
symmetric real matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
is positive semidefinite if
\bm{v}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\bm{\mathrm{A}}\bm{v}\geq 0
for all nonzero
\bm{v}\in\mathds{R}^{N}
.
We use this definition to prove that
\bm{\Gamma}
is a positive-semidefinite matrix.
Let
\bm{v}
be a nonzero vector in
\mathds{R}^{N}
and let
f(x):=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}v_{n}s_{n}(x)
, then

\bm{v}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\bm{\Gamma}\bm{v}=\sum_{n,m=0}^{N-1}v_{n}\Gamma_{nm}v_{m}=\int\differential{x}\derivative{x}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}v_{n}s_{n}(x)\right)\derivative{x}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}v_{m}s_{m}(x)\right)=\int\differential{x}\left(\derivative{x}f(x)\right)^{2}\geq 0,

(155)

Thus, by definition,
\bm{\Gamma}
is a positive-semidefinite matrix.
∎

Proposition D.2
.

Let
m_{0}\in\mathds{R}^{+}
be the free mass and
\bm{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}
be the ground-state ICM in a wavelet basis.
Then the smallest eigenvalue of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
is
m_{0}
.

Proof.

The spectrum of the ground-state ICM in a fixed- and multi-scale wavelet basis are identical because they are unitarily equivalent.
Therefore we only prove this proposition for a fixed-scale ICM
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(k)}_{\text{ss}}
(
24
).
This matrix is the principal square root of the fixed-scale coupling matrix in
eq.

23
.
The fixed-scale coupling matrix can be written as

\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(k)}_{\text{ss}}=m_{0}^{2}\mathds{1}-4^{k}\bm{\Delta}^{(2)}=m_{0}^{2}\mathds{1}+4^{k}\bm{\Gamma},

(156)

where
\bm{\Delta}^{(2)}
is a matrix whose elements are the second-order derivative overlaps
\Delta^{(2)}_{mn}
(
13
), and the second identity in
eq.

156
follows from
eq.

154
and
eq.

13
.
Eigenvalues of
\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(k)}_{\text{ss}}
are

\lambda^{(k)}_{j}:=m_{0}^{2}-4^{k}\Delta^{(2)}_{0}-2\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}4^{k}\Delta^{(2)}_{\ell}\cos\left(\frac{2\uppi\ell}{N}j\right),

(157)

which yields

\lambda^{(k)}_{0}=m_{0}^{2}-4^{k}\Delta^{(2)}_{0}-2\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}4^{k}\Delta^{(2)}_{\ell}=m^{2}_{0},

(158)

where the second identity follows from properties of the second-order derivative overlaps
(
Singh and Brennen 2016
, Appendix A)
.
By Eqs. (
156
), (
158
) and
proposition

D.1
,
m^{2}_{0}
is the smallest eigenvalue of the coupling matrix and therefore
m_{0}
is the smallest eigenvalue of the ground-state ICM in both fixed- and multi-scale wavelet basis.
∎

Proposition D.3

(Bound on determinant of near-identity matrices
Brent et al. 2015
)
.

Let
N\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
,
\varepsilon\in[0,1)
and
\bm{\mathrm{A}}=\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{E}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}
such that
\absolutevalue*{E_{ij}}\leq\varepsilon
for all

i,j\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
.
If
N\varepsilon\leq 1
, then

1-N\varepsilon\leq\det(\upbm{A})\leq(1-N\varepsilon)^{-1}.

(159)

Proposition D.4
.

Let
J\in 2\mathds{Z}^{+},\,\sigma\in\mathds{R}^{+}
and
\delta\leq\min(1/2,\sigma)
.
Define
r:=\delta/(\sigma\sqrt{2})
and

\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\int_{\mathds{R}}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}=\sigma\sqrt{2\uppi},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}:=\delta^{2}\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}.

(160)

Then

\dfrac{1}{\mathcal{N}\tilde{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\delta\,\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}^{2}}\int_{-J\delta/2}^{J\delta/2}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}.

(161)

Proof.

Let

p:=1+\mathrm{e}^{\uppi-2\uppi^{2}}\left(\frac{\uppi^{1/4}}{\Gamma(3/4)}-1\right),

(162)

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function.
We first prove that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{N}}\leq\sqrt{p\delta}\mathcal{N},

(163)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\delta/p}\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\geq\int_{-J\delta/2}^{J\delta/2}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}.

(164)

proposition

D.4
follows from Eqs. (
163
) and (
164
);

eq.

163
implies that
\sqrt{p\delta}/(\tilde{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{N})\geq 1/\mathcal{N}^{2}
and multiplying each side of this inequality by each side of
eq.

164
yields
eq.

161
.
We now prove
eq.

163
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\leq\sum_{j\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{\uppi}}{r}\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-k^{2}\uppi^{2}/r^{2}},

(165)

where the equality follows from the Poisson summation formula
(
Altland and von
Delft 2019
, p. 385)
.
We now find an upper bound for the right-hand side of
eq.

165
.
Note that
r^{2}\leq 1/2
so
\mathrm{e}^{-k^{2}\uppi^{2}/r^{2}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-2k^{2}\uppi^{2}}
,
also
\mathrm{e}^{-2k^{2}\uppi^{2}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\uppi-2\uppi^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi k^{2}}

for each
k\in\mathds{Z}/\{0\}
, therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-k^{2}\uppi^{2}/r^{2}}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-2k^{2}\uppi^{2}}\leq 1+\mathrm{e}^{\uppi-2\uppi^{2}}\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}/\{0\}}\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi k^{2}}=1+\mathrm{e}^{\uppi-2\uppi^{2}}\left(\frac{\uppi^{1/4}}{\Gamma(3/4)}-1\right)=p,

(166)

where we used
\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi k^{2}}=\uppi^{1/4}/\Gamma(3/4)

(
Berndt 1991
, p. 103)
.
By Eqs. (
160
), (
165
) and (
166
)

\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}=\delta^{2}\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\leq p\delta\mathcal{N}^{2},

(167)

which yields
eq.

163
.
We now prove
eq.

164
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\delta/p}\sum_{j=-J/2}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}=\sqrt{\delta/p}\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{-J^{2}r^{2}/4}+2\sum_{j=1}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\right),

(168)

and, as
\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}
is a convex function for
\absolutevalue*{x}\geq\sigma
,

\displaystyle\int_{-J\delta/2}^{J\delta/2}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\delta}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}+2\sum_{j=1}^{J/2-1}\int_{j\delta}^{(j+1)\delta}\differential{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+2\delta\sum_{j=1}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}-2(1/2)\delta\left((1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}})-\mathrm{e}^{-J^{2}r^{2}/4}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta\left((2-1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}})+\mathrm{e}^{-J^{2}r^{2}/4}+2\sum_{j=1}^{J/2-1}\mathrm{e}^{-j^{2}r^{2}}\right).

(169)

Note that if
\delta\leq(2-1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}})^{-2}/p=0.514998

then
\delta^{2}(2-1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}})^{2}\leq\delta/p
and therefore,

\displaystyle\delta(2-1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}})\leq\sqrt{\delta/p}.

(170)

This inequality implies that the right-hand side of
eq.

168
is greater than or equal to the right-hand side of
appendix

D
.
Therefore, Eqs. (
168
), (
D
), (
170
) and
\delta\leq 1/2
yields
eq.

164
.
∎

Proposition D.5
.

Let
d_{\max}\in\mathds{R}^{+}
and
d_{\min}\in\mathds{R}^{+}
be respectively the largest and the smallest diagonal elements of the diagonal matrix
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
in either the spectral or the UDU decomposition of a symmetric positive-definite matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}
, with
N\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
.
Also let
\kappa\in\mathds{R}^{+}
be the condition number of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
,
quantified as the ratio of the largest to the smallest eigenvalues of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
.
Then

\frac{d_{\max}}{d_{\min}}\in\order{\kappa}.

(171)

Proof.

If
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is the diagonal matrix in the spectral decomposition of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
,
then
d_{\max}
and
d_{\max}
are respectively the largest and the smallest eigenvalue of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
.
Therefore, by definition,

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\max}}{d_{\min}}=\kappa.

(172)

If
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is the diagonal matrix in the UDU decomposition of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\max}}{d_{\min}}\leq\kappa.

(173)

The proof of this inequality is as follows.
Let
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})
and
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})
be respectively the largest and the smallest eigenvalues of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
and let
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}
be the
i^{\text{th}}
column of the
N
-by-
N
identity matrix.
Then

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})=\max_{\norm{\bm{x}}=1}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathrm{A}}\bm{x}\geq\max_{i}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\bm{\mathrm{A}}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}=\max_{i}\left(\bm{\mathrm{UDU}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)_{ii}=\max_{i}\left(d_{i}+\sum_{j>i}d_{j}U^{2}_{ij}\right)\geq\max_{i}d_{i}=d_{\max},

(174)

\displaystyle\lambda^{-1}_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})=\lambda_{\max}\left(\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}\right)\geq\max_{i}\left(\bm{\mathrm{UDU^{\mathsf{T}}}}\right)^{-1}_{ii}=\max_{i}\left(d^{-1}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}d^{-1}_{j}\left(\bm{\mathrm{U}}^{-1}\right)^{2}_{ij}\right)\geq\max d^{-1}_{i}=d^{-1}_{\min}.

(175)

The second equality in
eq.

175
follows from the fact that the inverse of an upper unit-triangular matrix is an upper unit-triangular matrix
(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, p. 220)
.
The last inequality in Eqs. (
174
) and (
175
) holds because
d_{i}>0
for the positive-definite matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
.

proposition

D.5
follows from Eqs. (
172
) and (
173
).
∎

Proposition D.6
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathds{R}^{+}
be the condition number of the ICM for the ground state of a
N
-mode massive real scalar bosonic free QFT with mass
m_{0}\in\mathds{R}^{+}
in a fixed- or multi-scale wavelet basis.
Then

\kappa\in\Theta(N/m_{0}).

(176)

Proof.

Note that the ICM
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
in a multi-scale wavelet has the same condition number as the ICM
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}
in a fixed scale wavelet basis because
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
is obtained from
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}
by a wavelet transform.
We therefore consider the condition number of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}
.
Now using
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}=\sqrt{\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}}
, where
\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}
(
23
) is the coupling matrix in a fixed-scale wavelet basis, we have

\kappa=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})}{\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})}}.

(177)

We prove that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})=m^{2}_{0},

(178)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})\in\Theta(m^{2}_{0}+N^{2}).

(179)

proposition

D.6
follows form Eqs. (
177
) to (
179
).
To prove
eq.

179
, we find a lower and an upper bound for the largest eigenvalue of
\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}
.
We use the Gershgorin circle theorem to find the upper bound
(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, p. 388)
.
This theorem implies that

\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})\leq\max_{i}\left(K_{\text{ss};ii}+R_{i}\right),\;\;R_{i}:=\sum_{j\neq i}\absolutevalue*{K_{\sS;ij}}.

(180)

Note that for any positive-definite matrix
\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}

(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, p. 434)

\displaystyle\absolutevalue*{K_{\sS;ij}}^{2}\leq K_{\text{ss};ii}K_{\text{ss};jj},

(181)

and by
eq.

23

K_{\text{ss};\,ii}=m^{2}_{0}+N^{2}\Delta_{0}.

(182)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue*{K_{\sS;ij}}\leq m_{0}^{2}+N^{2}\Delta_{0}.

(183)

Furthermore,
\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}
is a banded circulant matrix with the lower and upper bandwidth
2\mathcal{K}-2
.
Thus, using
eq.

183
,

\displaystyle R_{i}\leq 2(2\mathcal{K}-2)(m_{0}^{2}+N^{2}\Delta_{0}).

(184)

Eqs. (
180
), (
182
) and (
184
) yield

\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})\leq(4\mathcal{K}-3)(m_{0}^{2}+N^{2}\Delta_{0}).

(185)

We now find a lower bound for the largest eigenvalue.
Note that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}})=\max_{\norm{\upbm{x}}=1}\bm{\mathrm{x}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}\bm{\mathrm{x}}\geq\max_{i}\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathsf{T}}_{i}\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}=m_{0}^{2}+N^{2}\Delta_{0},

(186)

where
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathds{1}_{N\times N}
and in the last equality we used
eq.

182
.
Eqs. (
185
) and (
186
) yield
eq.

179
.
∎

Proposition D.7

(exponentially decaying ICM at a fixed scale)
.

Let
m_{0}\in\mathds{R}^{+}
be the free mass,

\mathcal{K}\in\mathds{Z}_{\geq 3}
be the wavelet index and
r\in\mathds{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the scale index.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}^{(r)}\in\mathds{R}^{L2^{r}\times L2^{r}}
(
24
) with
L\in\mathds{Z}_{\geq 2(2\mathcal{K}-1)}
be the ground-state ICM at scale
r
.
Then,
for any
j

\absolutevalue{A^{(r)}_{\sS;\, 0, j}}\leq 4m_{0}\kappa^{(r)}2^{-\absolutevalue{j}/\xi^{(r)}},\quad\xi^{(r)}:=(2\mathcal{K}-1)2^{r+1}/m_{0},

(187)

where
\kappa^{(r)}>1
is the spectral condition number of
\bm{\mathrm{K}}_{\text{ss}}^{(r)}
(
23
).

Proof.

We employ the Benzi-Golub theorem
(
Benzi and Golub 1999
, Theorem 2.2)
to bound off-diagonal entries of ICM
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\text{ss}}^{(r)}
; see
(
Benzi 2016
, p. 243)
for an alternative statement of this theorem.
Let
\alpha>1
and
\beta>0
, with
\alpha>\beta
, be the half axes of an ellipse in the complex plane with foci in
\pm 1
.
This ellipse is specified by the sum of its half axes
\chi:=\alpha+\beta>1
, so we denote the ellipse by
{\cal{E}}_{\chi}
.
Let
f(z)
be an analytic function in the interior of
{\cal{E}}_{\chi}
and continuous on
{\cal{E}}_{\chi}
for any
1<\chi<\bar{\chi}
, and let

M(\chi):=\max_{z\in{{\cal{E}}_{\chi}}}\absolutevalue{f(z)}\quad\text{and}\quad K:=\frac{2\chi M(\chi)}{\chi-1}.

(188)

Also let
\bm{\mathrm{B}}
be a symmetric and banded matrix whose spectrum
\operatorname{spec}\bm{\mathrm{B}}

is contained in
[-1,1]
and let
\cal{B}
the upper bandwidth of
\bm{\mathrm{B}}
.
Then by the Benzi-Golub theorem
(
Benzi and Golub 1999
, Theorem 2.2)

\absolutevalue{\left[f(\upbm{B})\right]_{i,j}}\leq K2^{-\gamma\absolutevalue{i-j}},\quad\gamma:=\frac{\log_{2}{\chi}}{{\cal{B}}+1}.

(189)

The ICM matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
is the principal square root of the coupling matrix
\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
(
23
), and
\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
is a symmetric and banded matrix with the upper bandwidth
2\mathcal{K}-2
.
However,

\operatorname{spec}\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}

is not contained in
[-1,1]
, so we cannot directly apply the Benzi-Golub theorem to this matrix.
Let
[a,b]\subset\mathds{R}^{+}
be the interval containing
\operatorname{spec}\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
.
To apply the Benzi-Golub theorem, by shifting and scaling the coupling matrix, we construct the matrix

\bm{\mathrm{B}}:=\frac{2\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}-(b+a)\mathds{1}}{b-a},

(190)

whose spectrum is contained in
[-1,1]
.
Then the function

f(z):=\sqrt{\frac{b-a}{2}z+\frac{b+a}{2}},

(191)

maps
\bm{\mathrm{B}}
to the ICM; that is
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}=f(\bm{\mathrm{B}})
.
By the Benzi-Golub theorem, we then have

\absolutevalue{A^{(r)}_{\sS;\, 0, j}}\leq K2^{-\gamma\absolutevalue{j}},

(192)

with

\gamma=\frac{\log_{2}{\chi}}{2\mathcal{K}-1}.

(193)

We now obtain a lower bound for
\gamma
.
The function
f(z)
in
eq.

191
is analytic in the interior of any ellipse
{\cal{E}}_{\chi}
with
\chi
less than

\bar{\chi}=\frac{b+a}{b-a}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{b+a}{b-a}\right)^{2}-1}.

(194)

Using the spectral condition number
\kappa^{(r)}:=b/a
of
\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
, we have

\bar{\chi}=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}}+1}{\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}}-1}=1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}}-1}\geq 1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}-1}},

(195)

where the inequality holds because
\kappa^{(r)}\geq 1
.
Setting

\chi=1+2/\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}-1}\leq\bar{\chi},

(196)

we obtain

\log_{2}(\chi)=\log_{2}\left(1+2/\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}-1}\right)\geq\sqrt{2/\kappa^{(r)}}\geq m_{0}/2^{r+1},

(197)

where the first inequality holds because
\kappa^{(r)}\geq 1
and the second inequality holds because

\kappa^{(r)}\leq 1+4^{r+2}/m_{0}^{2}\leq 2\times 4^{r+2}/m_{0}^{2},

(198)

as per
proposition

D.6
.
The combination of
eq.

193
and
eq.

197
yields

\gamma\geq 1/\xi^{(r)},

(199)

where
\xi^{(r)}
is given in
eq.

187
.
We now find an upper bound for
K
in
eq.

192
.
By
eq.

196
and
\kappa^{(r)}\geq 1

\frac{2\chi}{\chi-1}=\chi\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}-1}=\sqrt{\kappa^{(r)}-1}+2\leq 2\sqrt{2\kappa^{(r)}}.

(200)

The function
f(z)
in
eq.

191
attains its maximum at the point
z_{0}:=\alpha
, where
\alpha
is the greater half axis of the ellipse
{\cal{E}}_{\chi}
.
The combination of
\chi=\alpha+\beta
,
\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}=1
and
eq.

196
yields

z_{0}:=\frac{\chi^{2}+1}{2\chi}=1+\frac{(\chi-1)^{2}}{2\chi}\leq 1+\frac{1}{2}(\chi-1)^{2}=1+\frac{2}{\kappa^{(r)}-1},

(201)

and

M(\chi)=\max_{z\in{\cal{E}}_{\chi}}\absolutevalue{f(z)}=f(z_{0})=\sqrt{\frac{b-a}{2}\frac{\chi^{2}+1}{2\chi}+\frac{b+a}{2}}\leq\sqrt{a/2}\sqrt{2\kappa^{(r)}+2}\leq\sqrt{2a\kappa^{(r)}}=m_{0}\sqrt{2\kappa^{(r)}},

(202)

where the last equality follows because the smallest eigenvalue of
\bm{\mathrm{K}}^{(r)}_{\text{ss}}
is
a=m_{0}^{2}
.
Eqs. (
188
), (
200
) and (
202
) yield

K\leq 4m_{0}\kappa^{(r)}.

(203)

proposition

D.7
follows from Eqs. (
192
), (
199
), and (
203
).
∎

D.1
Proof of
proposition

4.5

To prove
proposition

4.5
,
first we show that the
\varepsilon_{\text{th}}
-approximate ICM
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
(
50
) is a positive-definite matrix.
Then we prove that the infidelity between
\ket{\G_N(\upbm{A})}
and
\ket{\G_N(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})}
is no greater than
\varepsilon
.

We use the Bauer-Fike theorem
(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, p. 405)
to show that
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
is a positive-definite matrix.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}
be a nonsingular matrix such that
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{A}}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}=\bm{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix and let
\bm{\mathrm{E}}:=\bm{\mathrm{A}}-\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
.
According to the Bauer-Fike theorem,
for any eigenvalue
\lambda(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}})
of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
, there is an eigenvalue
\lambda(\bm{\mathrm{A}})
of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
such that

\absolutevalue{\lambda(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})-\lambda(\upbm{A})}\leq\kappa_{p}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}})\norm{\upbm{E}}_{p}\,,

(204)

where
\norm{\bullet}_{p}
is the matrix norm induced by any
p
-norm on
\mathds{C}^{N}
and
\kappa_{p}(\bullet)
is the condition number of a matrix
with respect to this norm.
As
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
is a real-symmetric matrix,
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}
can be chosen to be an orthogonal matrix for which
\norm{\upbm{Q}}_{2}=1
and
\kappa_{2}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}})=1
.
Therefore, using
eq.

204
with
p=2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\absolutevalue*{\lambda_{\min}(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})-\lambda(\upbm{A})}\leq\norm{\upbm{E}}_{2}\leq N\varepsilon_{\text{th}},

(205)

for the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{A}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}})
of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
.
The last inequality here comes from
\absolutevalue{E_{ij}}\leq\varepsilon_{\text{th}}
by the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{E}}
and using the matrix-norm inequalities

\norm{\upbm{E}}_{2}\leq\norm{\upbm{E}}_{\text{F}}\leq N\max_{i,j}\absolutevalue{E_{ij}}
, where
\norm{\bullet}_{\text{F}}
is the Frobenius norm of a matrix.
If
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}})\geq\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})
, then
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
is already a positive-definite matrix.
Otherwise, using
eq.

205

\displaystyle\absolutevalue*{\lambda_{\min}(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})-\lambda_{\min}(\upbm{A})}\leq N\varepsilon_{\text{th}}\leq\varepsilon\gamma/\sqrt{N},

(206)

which implies that the
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}
is a positive-definite matrix.

We now prove Eq. (
52
).
Let
\bm{\mathrm{T}}:=\bm{\mathrm{E}}\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}
, then

\innerproduct{\G_N(\upbm{A})}{\G_N(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})}=\left(\dfrac{\det\bm{\mathrm{A}}\det\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}}{(2\uppi)^{2N}}\right)^{1/4}\int_{\mathds{R}^{N}}\differential[N]{\bm{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\tfrac{1}{4}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\bm{\mathrm{A}}+\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}\right)\bm{x}}=\dfrac{\left[\det\left({\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}}\right)\right]^{1/4}}{\left[\det\left({\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}/2}\right)\right]^{1/2}},

(207)

where we use
\det\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}=\det\bm{\mathrm{A}}\det(\mathds 1-\upbm{T})
by employing the multiplicative property of the determinant function
(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, p. 11)
, and the Gaussian integral

\int_{\mathds{R}^{N}}\differential[N]{\bm{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathrm{A}}\bm{x}}=\left(\frac{(2\uppi)^{N}}{\det\bm{\mathrm{A}}}\right)^{1/2},

(208)

for a symmetric and positive-definite matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}
.
We now use
proposition

D.3
to obtain a lower and an upper bound for determinant of the near-identity matrix
\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}
.
Let us first find an upper bound for
\absolutevalue*{T_{ij}}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{T_{ij}}=\absolutevalue{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1} E_{ik}\left(\upbm{A}^{-1}\right)_{kj}}\leq\varepsilon_{\text{th}}\max_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\absolutevalue{\left(\upbm{A}^{-1}\right)_{kj}}=\varepsilon_{\text{th}}\norm{\upbm{A}^{-1}}_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{\text{th}}\gamma^{-1}\sqrt{N},

(209)

where the last inequality follows from the matrix-norm relations

\displaystyle\norm{\upbm{A}}_{1}\leq\sqrt{N}\norm{\upbm{A}}_{2},\;\;\norm{\upbm{A}}_{2}=\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}),

(210)

for a real-symmetric matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}

(
Horn and Johnson 2012
, pp. 346, 363)
,
and
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})=\lambda^{-1}_{\min}(\bm{\mathrm{A}})\leq\gamma^{-1}
;
note that
\gamma>0
is a lower bound for the eigenvalues of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
.
By
proposition

D.3
and
eq.

209
,
if
\varepsilon_{\text{th}}\gamma^{-1}N^{3/2}\leq\varepsilon
, then

\det\left(\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon,\;\;\det\left(\mathds{1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}/2\right)\leq(1-\varepsilon/2)^{-1}.

(211)

Combination of
eq.

207
and
eq.

211
yields

\innerproduct{\G_N(\upbm{A})}{\G_N(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})}\geq(1-\varepsilon)^{1/4}(1-\varepsilon/2)^{1/2}\geq 1-\varepsilon,

(212)

where, in the last inequality, we use
(1-\varepsilon/2)^{1/2}\geq(1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}
and
(1-\varepsilon)^{3/4}\geq 1-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Therefore, the infidelity between
\ket{\G_N(\upbm{A})}
and
\ket{\G_N(\upbm{A}_{\varepsilon_\text{th}})}
is at most
\varepsilon
.

D.2
Proof of
proposition

4.6

This appendix presents a proof for
proposition

4.6
.
Following
eq.

59
with
r=c
, we have

\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(r,r)}_{\text{ww}}=\bm{\mathrm{G}}\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(r+1)}_{\text{ss}}\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathsf{T}},

(213)

where
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is the lower half of the wavelet-transform matrix at scale
r
, see
eq.

147
, with entries
G_{nm}=g_{n-2m}
.
Using
eq.

213
and circulant property of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{(r+1)}_{\text{ss}}
, we have

A^{(r,r)}_{\text{ww};\,0,j}=\sum_{m,n=0}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}g_{n}g_{m}A^{(r+1)}_{\text{ss};\,0,(2j+m-n)}.

(214)

By virtue of
proposition

D.7
,
\absolutevalue{A^{(r+1)}_{\sS;\, 0,j}}
decreases as
j
increases.
For any
m,n
and
j\geq 2\mathcal{K}-1
, we have
2j+m-n\geq j
, so

\absolutevalue{A^{(r+1)}_{\sS;\, 0, (2j+m-n)}}\leq\absolutevalue{A^{(r+1)}_{\sS;\, 0,j}}\quad\forall j\geq 2\mathcal{K}-1.

(215)

eq.

214
and
eq.

215
yield

\absolutevalue{A^{(r,r)}_{\ww;\, 0, j}}\leq\absolutevalue{A^{(r+1)}_{\sS;\, 0,j}}\sum_{m,n=0}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}\absolutevalue{g_n g_m},

(216)

and because
\sum g^{2}_{n}=1
, we have
\absolutevalue{g_n}\leq 1
.
Therefore

\sum_{m,n=0}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}\absolutevalue{g_n g_m}\leq\left(\sum_{m=0}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}\absolutevalue{g_m}\right)\left(\sum_{n=0}^{2\mathcal{K}-1}\absolutevalue{g_n}\right)<4\mathcal{K}^{2}.

(217)

proposition

4.6
follows from Eqs. (
187
), (
216
) and (
217
).

D.3
Proof of
corollary

4.7

Here we prove
corollary

4.7
.
By virtue of
proposition

4.6
, the off-diagonal entries in diagonal blocks of the multi-scale ICM decay slower as the scale index
r
increases.
Therefore, for a given threshold value
\varepsilon_{\text{th}}
, the bottom-right block of the approximate ICM
eq.

50
has the largest bandwidth among the diagonal blocks.
Plugging
r=k-1
into
eq.

53
we obtain

\absolutevalue{A^{(k-1,k-1)}_{\ww;\, 0, j}}\leq\varepsilon_{\text{th}},

(218)

for

\absolutevalue{j}\geq\xi^{(k)}\log_{2}\left(\frac{16\mathcal{K}m_{0}\kappa^{(k)}}{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}\right).

(219)

We now use
proposition

4.5
and
2^{k+1}\leq\log N
to express the upper bandwidth in terms of
\mathcal{K},m_{0},\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}
and
N
.
By
eq.

53

\xi^{(k)}=(2\mathcal{K}-1)2^{k+1}/m_{0}\leq(2\mathcal{K}-1)\left(\log N\right)/m_{0}.

(220)

The smallest eigenvalue of the ICM is
m_{0}
, so by
proposition

4.5
we take

\varepsilon_{\text{th}}=m_{0}\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}N^{-3/2}
.
Therefore

\frac{16\mathcal{K}^{2}m_{0}\kappa^{(k)}}{\varepsilon_{\text{th}}}=\frac{16\mathcal{K}^{2}N^{3/2}\kappa^{(k)}}{\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}}\leq\frac{32\mathcal{K}^{2}N^{3/2}4^{k+2}}{m^{2}_{0}\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}}\leq\frac{32\mathcal{K}^{2}N^{3/2}\log^{2}N}{m_{0}^{2}\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}}\leq\frac{16\mathcal{K}^{2}N^{2}}{m_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}_{\text{vac}}},

(221)

where we use
eq.

198
in the first inequality.
In the last two inequalities we use

2^{k+1}\leq\log N,\quad\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}\geq\varepsilon^{2}_{\text{vac}}\quad\text{and}\quad 32N^{3/2}\log^{2}N\leq 16N^{2},

(222)

for
\varepsilon_{\text{vac}}\in(0,1)
and
N\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
.
Equation
54
follows from
eqs.

219
to
221
.

Appendix E
Computing rotation angle for 1DG-state generation

In this appendix, we analyze the time complexity for computing the rotation angle
\theta_{\ell}
in
eq.

71
on a quantum computer.
We first simplify computing
\theta_{\ell}
using the double-angle identity as

\theta_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\arccos(u_\ell)=\frac{\uppi}{4}-\frac{1}{4}\arcsin(u_\ell),

(223)

where

u_{\ell}:=2\dfrac{f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}/2,\,\mu_{\ell}/2,\,m_{\ell}-1\right)}{f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)}-1,

(224)

and
f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)
is defined in
eq.

69
.
Using
eq.

223
, computing
\theta_{\ell}
requires computing
u_{\ell}
and
\arcsin(u_\ell)
, and performing one multiplication and one addition.
Therefore, time complexity for computing the rotation angle
\theta_{\ell}
, denoted by
T_{\textsc{angle}}
, is

T_{\textsc{angle}}=T_{u}+T_{\arcsin}+2,

(225)

where
T_{u}
and
T_{\arcsin}
are time complexities for computing
u_{\ell}
and
\arcsin(u_\ell)
, respectively.
By
eq.

224
, computing
u_{\ell}
requires computing
f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}/2,\,\mu_{\ell}/2,\,m_{\ell}-1\right)
and

f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)
, and performing one division, one multiplication and one addition.
Therefore,

T_{u}=2T_{f}+3,

(226)

where
T_{f}
is time complexity for computing
f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)
.
By
eq.

225
and
eq.

226
, we only need
T_{f}
and
T_{\arcsin}
to obtain
T_{\textsc{angle}}
.
First we provide a high-level description of how to obtain
T_{f}
and
T_{\arcsin}
.
We show, in
proposition

E.1
, that computing
f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)
to
t
bit of precision requires computing at most
4t-1
exponentials and adding them all.
The argument of each exponential needs performing one addition, one multiplication, one division and calculating the square of two numbers.
Calculating the square of a number can be performed by one multiplication
(
Häner et al. 2018
, p. 7)
.
Altogether, time complexity for computing
f\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell},\,m_{\ell}\right)
is at most
(4t-1)T_{\exp}+(4t-2)+5
, and therefore

T_{f}\in\order{tT_{\exp}},

(227)

where
T_{\exp}
is time complexity for computing an exponential.
We use
proposition

E.2
to show
T_{\exp}
scales linearly with
t
.
By this proposition, to compute
\mathrm{e}^{-x}
with
t
bits of precision, first the polynomial

P(x):=a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2},

(228)

with
a_{0}=1,a_{1}=1/4^{t}
and
a_{2}=1/4^{2t}
is evaluated at
x
and then the result is iteratively squared
2t
times.
Computing square of a number needs one multiplication, so
T_{\exp}=T_{P}+2t
, where
T_{P}
is time complexity for computing
P(x)
in
eq.

228
.

We use Horner’s method to compute a polynomial on a quantum computer
Häner et al. 2018
, and to obtain
T_{P}
.
To evaluate
the polynomial in
eq.

228
at
x
, we store the coefficients
a_{0},a_{1}
and
a_{2}
on a ancillary quantum register and implement the operation

a_{2}x+a_{1}\mapsto(a_{2}x+a_{1})x+a_{0}
.
Implementing this operation needs two multiplications and two additions before uncomputing the intermediate results, so
T_{P}
is a constant and

T_{\exp}\in\Theta(t).

(229)

We use
proposition

E.3
and
proposition

E.4
to obtain
T_{\arcsin}
.
In
proposition

E.3
, we show that the argument
x
of
\arcsin(x)
is a positive number less than
1/2
and, in
proposition

E.4
,
we show a polynomial of degree
t

suffices to approximate
\arcsin(x)
with
t
bits of precision.
As described, computing a polynomial of degree
t
by Horner’s method requires
\Theta(t)
multiplications and additions, so

T_{\arcsin}=\Theta(t),

(230)

The combination of
eqs.

225
to
227
and
eqs.

229
to
230
yields

T_{\textsc{angle}}\in\order{t^2}.

(231)

We now state and prove the propositions that we use to obtain
T_{\textsc{angle}}
.

Proposition E.1
.

Let
m\in\mathds{Z}^{+},t\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
,
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
,
\mu_{\ell}\in[0,1)
,
m_{\ell}:=m-\ell
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\in(0,2^{m_{\ell}/2})
and

\displaystyle f(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\mu_{\ell},m_{\ell}):=\sum_{j=-2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{2^{m_{\ell}-1}-1}\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right),

(232)

\displaystyle g(\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell},\mu_{\ell}):=\sum_{j=-2t-1}^{2t}\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right).

(233)

For
2^{m_{\ell}}>8(t+3)
, if
\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}>t
then

\absolutevalue{f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)-\tilde{\sigma}_\ell\sqrt{2\uppi}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{t+1}},

(234)

else

\absolutevalue{f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)-g(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_\ell)}\leq\frac{1}{2^{t+1}}.

(235)

Proof.

We first prove the first part of
proposition

E.1
.
Using the triangle inequality,

\absolutevalue{
f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)
-\sigma_\ell\sqrt{2\uppi}
}\leq\absolutevalue{
f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)
-\sum_{j\in\integers}
\exp\left(
-\tfrac{(j+\mu_\ell)^2}{2\tilde{\sigma}_\ell^2}
\right)
}+\absolutevalue{\sum_{j\in\integers}
\exp\left(
-\tfrac{(j+\mu_\ell)^2}{2\tilde{\sigma}_\ell^2}
\right)
- \tilde{\sigma}_\ell\sqrt{2\uppi}}.

(236)

Let us now find and upper bound for the first term in the right-hand side of
eq.

236
.
By
eq.

232
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)
-\sum_{j\in\integers}
\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_\ell)^2}{2\tilde{\sigma}_\ell^2}\right)
}

\displaystyle=\sum_{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{\infty}\left(\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)+\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+1-\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\tfrac{j^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\leq 2\sum_{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-j/4}=\dfrac{2\mathrm{e}^{-2^{m_{\ell}-3}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-1/4}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t}.

(237)

The first inequality here holds because
\mu_{\ell}\in[0,1)
.
The second inequality is obtained using

\sum_{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\tfrac{j^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\leq\sum_{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\tfrac{2^{m_{\ell}-1}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}j\right),

(238)

and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}\leq 2^{m_{\ell}}
.
The last inequality is obtained using
2^{m_{\ell}}>8(t+3)
and
2\mathrm{e}^{-3}/(1-\mathrm{e}^{-1/4})<1/2
.

We now establish an upper bound for the second term in the right-hand side of
eq.

236
.
By Poisson’s summation formula
(
Altland and von
Delft 2019
, p. 385)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathds{Z}}\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)=\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\sqrt{2\uppi}\sum_{j\in\mathds{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j^{2}-2\uppi\mathrm{i}\mu_{\ell}j}=\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\sqrt{2\uppi}\left(1+2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j^{2}}\cos(2\uppi\mu_\ell j)\right).

(239)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\sum_{j\in\integers}
\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_\ell)^2}{2\tilde{\sigma}_\ell^2}\right)
- \tilde{\sigma}_\ell\sqrt{2\uppi}}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2\uppi}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\absolutevalue{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
\e^{-2\uppi^2\tilde{\sigma}_\ell^2j^2}
\cos(2\uppi\mu_\ell j)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2\uppi}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2\uppi}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j}=2\sqrt{2\uppi}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi^{2}t}.

(240)

Here we use the triangle inequality and
\absolutevalue{\cos(2\uppi\mu_\ell j)}\leq 1
to obtain the first inequality.
The second inequality is obtained by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j^{2}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}j}.

(241)

The third inequality is obtained using
1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\uppi^{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}
for
\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}\geq t\geq 1
,
and
2\sqrt{2\uppi}x\mathrm{e}^{-(\uppi^{2}-1)x^{2}}\leq 1
for any
x\in\mathds{R}^{+}
.
The last inequality holds because

\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}\geq t
.
The first part of
proposition

E.1
follows from Eqs. (
236
), (
E
), (
E
) and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\mathrm{e}^{-\uppi^{2}t}\leq\frac{1}{2^{t+1}}\;\;\;\forall t\in\mathds{Z}^{+}.

(242)

We now prove the second part of
proposition

E.1
.
By Eqs.(
232
) and (
233
),

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{f(\tilde{\sigma}_\ell,\mu_\ell,m_\ell)-g(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2t+1}^{2^{m_{\ell}-1}-1}\left(\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)+\exp\left(-\tfrac{(j+1-\mu_{\ell})^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=2t+1}^{2^{m_{\ell}-1}-1}\exp\left(-\tfrac{j^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\leq 2\sum_{j=2t+1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-j}=2\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(2t+1)}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-1}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{t+1}}.

(243)

The first inequality here is obtained using
\mu_{\ell}\in[0,1)
; the second inequality is obtained
using

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2t+1}^{2^{m_{\ell}-1}-1}\exp\left(-\tfrac{j^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}\right)\leq\sum_{j=2t+1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\tfrac{2t+1}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}}j\right),

(244)

and

\tilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{2}\leq t
.
The last inequality is valid for any
t\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
.
∎

Proposition E.2
.

Let
t\in\mathds{Z}^{+},x\in[0,t]
and
P(x/\ell):=1-x/\ell+x^{2}/\ell^{2}
.
Then, for any
\ell\geq 4^{t}
,

\absolutevalue{\e^{-x}-P^\ell(x/\ell)}\leq 1/4^{t}.

(245)

Proof.

Note that

\mathrm{e}^{-x}=\left(\mathrm{e}^{-x/\ell}\right)^{\ell}=\left[P(x/\ell)+E(x/\ell)\right]^{\ell},\quad E(x/\ell):=\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{(-)^{n}}{n!}(x/\ell)^{n}.

(246)

Using binomial expansion

\mathrm{e}^{-x}=P^{\ell}(x/\ell)+\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{n}P^{\ell-n}(x/\ell)E^{n}(x/\ell).

(247)

Therefore,

\absolutevalue{\e^{-x}-P^{\ell}(x/\ell)}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{n}\absolutevalue{P(x/\ell)}^{\ell-n}\absolutevalue{E(x/\ell)}^{n}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{n}\absolutevalue{P(x/\ell)}^{\ell-n}\absolutevalue{E(x/\ell)}^{n},

(248)

where, in the last inequality, we use
P(x/\ell)\leq 1
for
x/\ell\leq 1
.
Let us now find an upper bound for
\absolutevalue{E(x/\ell)}
.
Using
eq.

246
and the triangle inequality,

\absolutevalue{E(x/\ell)}\leq\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{1}{n!}(x/\ell)^{n}=(x/\ell)^{3}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{(n+3)!}(x/\ell)^{n}\leq\frac{1}{6}(x/\ell)^{3}\mathrm{e}^{x/\ell}\leq\frac{1}{2}(x/\ell)^{3},

(249)

the last inequality holds because
\mathrm{e}^{x/\ell}/3\leq 1
for
x/\ell\leq 1
.
Using Eqs. (
248
) and (
249
)

\absolutevalue{\e^{-x}-P^{\ell}(x/\ell)}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{n}\frac{1}{2^{n}}(x/\ell)^{3n}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\frac{\ell^{n}}{2^{n}}(x/\ell)^{3n}=\frac{x^{3}}{2\ell^{2}}+\sum_{n=2}^{\ell}\frac{x^{3n}}{2^{n}\ell^{2n}}\leq\frac{x^{3}}{2\ell^{2}}\left(1+\frac{x^{3}}{2\ell^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\ell}\leq\frac{1}{4^{t}}.

(250)

Here we use
x^{3}/\ell\leq 1/2
for
x\in[0,t]
and
\ell>4^{t}
.
∎

Proposition E.3
.

Let
\tilde{\sigma}\in[1,\infty),\mu\in[0,1),m\in\mathds{Z}^{+}
and

f(\tilde{\sigma},\mu,m)=\sum_{j=-2^{m-1}}^{2^{m-1}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(j+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}.

(251)

Then

\absolutevalue{2\frac{f(\tilde{\sigma}/2,\mu/2,m-1)}{f(\tilde{\sigma},\mu,m)}-1}\leq 1/2.

(252)

Proof.

Let
f_{\text{even}}:=f(\tilde{\sigma}/2,\mu/2,m-1)
and
f_{\text{odd}}=f(\tilde{\sigma}/2,(\mu+1)/2,m-1)
.
Then
f(\tilde{\sigma},\mu,m)=f_{\text{even}}+f_{\text{odd}}
, and
eq.

252
becomes

\absolutevalue{\frac{f_\text{even}-f_\text{odd}}{f_\text{even}+f_\text{odd}}}\leq 1/2.

(253)

We show
3f_{\text{even}}\geq f_{\text{odd}}
and
3f_{\text{odd}}\geq f_{\text{even}}
.
These two inequalities yield
eq.

253
which proves
proposition

E.3
.
Notice that

\displaystyle f_{\text{even}}=\sum_{j=0}^{2^{m-2}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{m-2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{2^{m-2}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\sum_{j=2}^{2^{m-2}+1}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-2-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}},

(254)

\displaystyle f_{\text{odd}}=\sum_{j=0}^{2^{m-2}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+1+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{m-2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{m-2}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-1+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right).

(255)

Using these equations

\displaystyle f_{\text{even}}-f_{\text{odd}}=\sum_{j=0}^{2^{m-2}-1}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+\mu+1)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)+\sum_{j=2}^{2^{m-2}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-2-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2^{m-1}-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}

(256)

\displaystyle f_{\text{odd}}-f_{\text{even}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{m-2}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-\mu-1)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{m-2}-1}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j-1+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2j+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2^{m-1}-1+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}.

(257)

The first three terms in the right-hand-side of these equations are non-negative, so we have

f_{\text{even}}-f_{\text{odd}}\geq-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}},\quad f_{\text{odd}}-f_{\text{even}}\geq-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}.

(258)

By these inequalities,
eq.

254
and
eq.

255

\displaystyle 3f_{\text{even}}-f_{\text{odd}}\geq 2f_{\text{even}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\geq 2\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\geq 0,

(259)

\displaystyle 3f_{\text{odd}}-f_{\text{even}}\geq 2f_{\text{odd}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\geq 2\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1+\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(1-\mu)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}\geq 0,

(260)

for all
\tilde{\sigma}\geq 1
and
\mu\in[0,1)
.
These inequalities yield

3f_{\text{even}}\geq f_{\text{odd}}

and

3f_{\text{odd}}\geq f_{\text{even}}
,
and hence
eq.

253
.
∎

Proposition E.4
.

Let
t\in\mathds{Z}^{+},\absolutevalue{x}\leq 1/2
and

P(x):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{t-1}a_{2\ell+1}x^{2\ell+1},\quad a_{2\ell+1}:=\dfrac{(2\ell-1)!!}{(2\ell)!!}\dfrac{1}{2\ell+1}.

(261)

Then
\absolutevalue{\arcsin(x)-P(x)}\leq 1/2^{2t+1}
.

Proof.

Using the binomial series of
(1-x^{2})^{-1/2}
,

\arcsin(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\differential{x}}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}=P(x)+\int_{0}^{x}\differential{x}\sum_{\ell=t}^{\infty}\frac{(2\ell-1)!!}{(2\ell)!!}x^{2\ell}.

(262)

By this equation and
(2\ell-1)!!/(2\ell)!!<1
we have

\absolutevalue{\arcsin(x)-P(x)}<\int_{0}^{x}\differential{x}\sum_{\ell=t}^{\infty}x^{2\ell}=\int_{0}^{x}\differential{x}x^{2t}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}x^{2\ell}\leq\frac{4}{3}\frac{x^{2t+1}}{2t+1}\leq\frac{1}{2^{2t+1}},

(263)

where we use
\absolutevalue{x}\leq 1/2
and
t\geq 1
in the last two inequalities.
∎

Appendix F
UDU decomposition

In this appendix, we present a classical algorithm for computing the UDU decomposition of a dense real-symmetric matrix.
The algorithm presented here elucidates our classical UDU-decomposition algorithm for a matrix with a fingerlike sparse structure.
First we describe the UDU matrix decomposition and then present the algorithm for a dense matrix as pseudocode.

In the UDU matrix decomposition, a symmetric matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
is decomposed into the product of an upper unit-triangular matrix
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
, a diagonal matrix
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
and transpose of the upper unit-triangular matrix.
The UDU decomposition is closely related to the LDL decomposition, where a symmetric matrix is decomposed into the product of a lower unit-triangular matrix
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
, a diagonal matrix and transpose of the lower unit-triangular matrix.
The LDL decomposition algorithm starts from the top-left corner of the matrix
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
and proceeds to compute entries of this matrix row by row
Higham 2009
.
The UDU-decomposition algorithm, however, starts from the top-right corner of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
and proceeds to compute its entries column by column.

To elucidate the algorithm, we write the UDU decomposition of a real-symmetric matrix
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
as

\bm{\mathrm{A}}=\bm{\mathrm{{UDU}}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{\mathrm{UV}}^{\mathsf{T}},\quad\bm{\mathrm{V}}:=\bm{\mathrm{UD}},

(264)

where
\bm{\mathrm{V}}
is an upper triangular matrix.
By definition, the nonzero elements in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\bm{\mathrm{V}}
are

\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,\,i:N-1}=\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{i,\,i:N-1}\odot\bm{\mathrm{d}}_{i:N-1},

(265)

where
\odot
denotes the Hadamard product.
By
eq.

264
, elements of the
i^{\text{th}}
column in the upper-triangular part of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
are

\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{0:i,\,i}=\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{0:i,\,i:N-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,\,i:N-1}.

(266)

This equation along with
u_{ii}=1
and
v_{ii}=d_{i}
yield

\displaystyle d_{i}=a_{ii}-\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{i,\,i+1:N-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,\,i+1:N-1},

(267)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{0:i-1,\,i}=\left[\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{0:i-1,i}-\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{0:i-1,\,i+1:N-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,\,i+1:N-1}\right]/d_{i}\quad\forall\,i\neq 0,

(268)

for the diagonal
d_{i}
and shear elements in the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
, respectively.

The procedure of the UDU-decomposition algorithm is as follows.
We start from the last column
i=N-1
and proceed to the first column
i=0
.
For each index
i
, first we compute
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,\,i:N-1}
, i.e., the nonzero elements in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\bm{\mathrm{V}}
by
eq.

266
.
Then we compute the
i^{\text{th}}
diagonal
d_{i}
of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
by
eq.

267
and the shear elements in the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
by
eq.

268
.
The inputs, outputs and explicit procedure of the algorithm is presented in
algorithm

12

Algorithm 12

Classical algorithm for UDU decomposition of a dense real-symmetric matrix

1:

2:

N\in\mathds{Z}^{+}

\triangleright
order of a square matrix

3:

\bm{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}

\triangleright
a real-symmetric matrix of order
N

4:

5:

\bm{\mathrm{U}}\in\mathds{R}^{N\times N}

\triangleright
upper unit-triangular matrix in UDU decomposition of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}

6:

\bm{\mathrm{d}}\in\mathds{R}^{N}

\triangleright
diagonals of the diagonal matrix
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
in UDU decomposition of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}

7:

function

UDU
(
N,\bm{\mathrm{A}}
)

8:

\bm{\mathrm{U}}\leftarrow\mathds{1}

\triangleright
initializes
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
as identity matrix

9:

for

i\leftarrow N-1
to
0

do

\triangleright
iterates over columns/rows of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}
from right/bottom to left/top

10:

\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,i+1:N-1}\leftarrow\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{i,i+1:N-1}\odot\bm{\mathrm{d}}_{i+1:N-1}

\triangleright

\odot
denotes the Hadamard product

11:

d_{i}\leftarrow a_{ii}-\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{i,i+1:N-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,i+1:N-1}

12:

if

i>0

then

13:

\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{0:i-1,i}\leftarrow\left[\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{0:i-1,i}-\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{0:i-1,i+1:N-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i,i+1:N-1}\right]/d_{i}

14:

return

\{\bm{\mathrm{d}},\bm{\mathrm{U}}\}
